--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score is minimized (lower/more negative = better)
- One half of source (BLUE wall) gives items that decrease score by 1 (good)
- Other half (GREEN wall) gives items that increase score by 1 (bad)
- Need to: go to the BLUE side, pick up items, return to nest (WHITE floor)

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green=2) with cyan LED → moves away from green walls
- State 1: GoToColor(yellow=4) with yellow LED → moves toward yellow objects
- Transition 0→1: sees magenta(5) with p=0.68
- Transition 1→0: on gray(1), 1→1: on white(2), 1→2: sees yellow(4)

Wait, let me re-read color codes: clr1=4 means yellow. The robot in state 1 goes toward yellow things. But what's yellow? The LEDs... if some robots have yellow LEDs, they'd be attracted to each other.

Actually the color codes for `--clr`: {1,2,3,4,5,6} = ? Looking at the arena: RED wall (nest side), BLUE walls (one source half), GREEN walls (other source half). Colors 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan probably.

So champion state 0: GoAwayColor(green) - avoids green source half. State 1: GoToColor(yellow) - goes toward yellow... but yellow is LED color. With cyan LEDs on state 0 and yellow LEDs on state 1, robots attract each other when in state 1.

The score of -10.02 is good. Let me focus on:
1. Elitism: copy champion exactly
2. Exploitation: small tweaks to champion
3. Exploration: new topologies targeting blue walls (color 3)

Key insight: GoToColor(blue=3) should direct robots to the good source. GoAwayColor(green=2) avoids bad source. On WHITE floor (nest), deliver item. 

**Machines:**

1. Champion (exact copy)
2-6. Exploitation variants of champion
7-14. Exploration variants

- State 0 → State 1 when: BLACK floor (p=0.9) OR sees cyan(6) (p=0.65)
- State 1 → State 0 when: WHITE floor (p=0.65) OR GRAY floor (p=0.4) OR sees cyan(6) (p=0.5)

State 0: GoAwayColor(green) with cyan LED → avoids green source, broadcasts cyan
State 1: GoToColor(red=1) with yellow LED → goes toward RED wall (nest side!)

So the behavior is: start avoiding green, when on black (source area) go to red wall (nest), when back at white/gray return to avoiding green. This makes sense for foraging from BLUE side!

**Key insight:** The robot avoids green (bad source), enters blue source (black floor triggers transition), then heads to red wall (nest), delivers, and cycles back.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Adjust probabilities on transitions
2. Try GoToColor(blue=3) instead of GoAwayColor(green=2) for state 0
3. Add more transitions to improve robustness
4. Try 3-state versions with explicit blue-seeking

**Color mapping guesses:** clr=1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

The champion works because: avoid green → enter blue (black floor) → go to red wall (nest) → deliver → repeat.

Let me design exploitation variants and exploration variants:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 6 --clr0 2 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.9 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --p0x1 0.65 --l0x1 3 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --cle1 4 --clr1 1 --n1 3 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.65 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.4 --n1x2 0 --c1x2 7 --p1x2 0.5 --l1x2 6

(Champion - exact copy)

Now exploitation variants:

1. Adjust black floor probability: 0.9 → 0.95
2. Adjust white floor return probability: 0.65 → 0.75
3. Adjust gray floor probability: 0.4 → 0.3
4. Add transition from state 0 on white floor back (already happening via black→1)
5. Try cyan sees trigger: 0.65 → 0.55 for blue(3)
6. 3-state version with explicit blue-seeking state

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Pattern analysis:**
The champion and top performers all share the same 2-state topology:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green=2), cyan LED, 2 transitions: BLACK floor (p≈0.85) → State 1, sees blue(3) (p≈0.7) → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(red=1), yellow LED, 3 transitions: WHITE floor (p≈0.65) → State 0, GRAY floor (p≈0.45) → State 0, sees cyan(6) (p≈0.45) → State 0

The scores are tightly clustered around -11.3. The topology is converging. Key observations:
1. The `l0x1 3` (sees blue walls) trigger works well
2. The `l1x2 6` (sees cyan LEDs of other robots) trigger works well
3. 3-state variants perform worse
4. The parameter space around the champion is fairly narrow

**Exploitation:** Fine-tune probabilities more carefully:
- p0x0 range: 0.75-0.90
- p0x1 range: 0.65-0.80
- p1x0 range: 0.60-0.75
- p1x1 range: 0.35-0.55
- p1x2 range: 0.35-0.55

**Exploration:** 
- Try adding a 4th transition to state 1 (the black floor one worked at -11.31)
- Try different combinations of transition conditions
- Try GoToColor(blue=3) as state 0 instead of GoAwayColor(green=2)
- Try swapping which transition triggers which state

The 4-transition state 1 version with black floor (p=0.05) scored -11.31, suggesting a small probability of re-entering source area from nest-seeking is useful.

## Reasoning

**Current champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green=2), cyan LED. Transitions: BLACK floor (p=0.85) → State 1, sees blue(3) (p=0.7) → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(red=1), yellow LED. 4 transitions: WHITE floor (p=0.7) → State 0, GRAY floor (p=0.4) → State 0, sees cyan(6) (p=0.45) → State 0, BLACK floor (p=0.06) → State 0

The 4th transition (black floor p=0.06) in state 1 is new and improved the score slightly. This makes sense: if the robot accidentally picks up the wrong item (green side), it can escape back.

**Key observations:**
1. The 2-state topology dominates consistently
2. Adding a 4th black-floor transition to state 1 helped (p=0.06)
3. p1x0 (white floor) seems best around 0.68-0.72
4. p1x1 (gray floor) around 0.38-0.45
5. p1x2 (sees cyan) around 0.45-0.50
6. Adding gray floor trigger to state 0 made it worse
7. 3-state versions generally underperform

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Fine-tune the 4-transition champion: vary p1x3 (black floor, currently 0.06) between 0.03-0.1
- Vary p0x0 (black floor trigger in state 0): 0.80-0.90
- Combine best p values from top performers
- Try p1x0=0.70, p1x1=0.40 with different p1x2 values

**Exploration:**
- Try a small random probability trigger instead of black floor for 4th transition
- Try adding 4th transition to state 0 as well
- Try color 5 (magenta) instead of cyan for sees trigger

## Reasoning

**Pattern analysis after 7 rounds:**

- p0x0=0.85, p0x1=0.70 (blue walls trigger)
- p1x0=0.70 (white), p1x1=0.40 (gray), p1x2=0.45 (cyan), p1x3=0.06 (black)

- p1x3=0.05 also works well (-11.40), p1x3=0.03 slightly worse (-11.16)
- p1x0=0.71, p1x1=0.39 slightly worse (-11.28)
- p1x0=0.69, p1x1=0.41 is worse (-10.60) - asymmetry matters
- Adding more transitions to state 0 is harmful
- The c1x3=7 (sees blue) trigger for 4th transition is worse than c=0 (black floor)

The champion seems near-optimal for this topology. We're stuck at -11.73 for multiple rounds. 

**What to try:**
1. Very fine perturbations: p1x3 in {0.055, 0.065, 0.07}
2. Try p1x0=0.70, p1x1=0.40 but vary p1x2 more carefully: 0.44, 0.46
3. Try 3-state extensions where state 2 is a brief exploration phase
4. Try changing the 4th transition type to C=1 (gray floor) instead of C=0 (black)
5. Try combining the p=0.05 and p=0.06 champion variants slightly differently
6. Try varying p0x0 more finely: 0.83, 0.84, 0.87

The search is converging - I should keep most machines very close to champion while a few explore genuinely different topologies.

## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**
This is the last round. The champion (-11.73) has been stable for rounds 5-9. Key findings:

1. **4th transition type C=7 (sees green=2) with p=0.2** scored -11.57 - new competitive variant
2. **p1x2=0.47** slightly worse than 0.45
3. **p0x1=0.69** slightly worse than 0.70
4. **p0x0=0.83** worse, 0.87 also worse → 0.85 is clearly optimal
5. **p1x3=0.07** scored -11.14, similar to champion
6. **C=1 (gray) p=0.10** was worst → avoid
7. **p1x2=0.43** was worst → avoid

**The C=7/l=2 (sees green) 4th transition** (p=0.2, -11.57) is interesting - it means if state 1 (going to nest) robot sees green walls, it returns to state 0 (avoid green). This makes behavioral sense: don't carry green items.

**Final strategy:**
- Elitism: champion copy
- Exploit: variations around champion with fine-tuned p1x3, p1x2, p0x1
- Exploit the new C=7/l=2 variant with different probabilities
- Try combining C=7/l=2 with slightly different main parameters
- A couple of genuinely different attempts

The search space is exhausted near the champion. Focus on resampling promising configurations to reduce stochastic noise, and a few new perturbations.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 12 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.46 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
